Introduction    Undoubtedly the best known collection of Buddhist texts in Sri Lanka is the Catubhāavārapāi, the Text of the Four Recitals.1 On any given day of the year one would not have to go very far to find a complete recital of these texts being made, usually by monks, in an all-night sitting, as the Buddhist community regards such a recital as being particularly auspicious, and believes it brings safety, peace, and well-being in its wake.  Following the Autumnal Rains Retreat (Vassa) every monastery and temple in the land has such a recital to ensure the prosperity of the temple and the community during the coming year; and throughout the year in the monasteries and temples up and down the land a selection of texts from this collection is recited to promote the safety and happiness of all those who attend such gatherings, and others to whom the chanting is dedicated to.   At auspicious times such as the inauguration of a new temple or home, or on merit-making occasions; and on inauspicious occasions such as a funeral, or an anniversary of the death of a loved one, there may also be a recitation of these discourses. Also in times of adversity, when ill-health or disease are close at hand, certain discourses from the collection will be recited which are thought to be particularly effective in restoring confidence and good health. Other discourses are employed when invisible forces or spirits are behaving antagonistically towards people; and at times certain of these discourses are recited as a blessing upon those who hear them.  In terms of the media it would be hard to find any other book in Sri Lanka that has so many editions available, and most homes in the Buddhist community will possess and prize a copy. The Great Safeguard, or Mahāparitta, which opens the recital has been recorded many times and can be heard morning and evening played over loudspeakers from homes and temples alike.  Enough then should have been said to give an idea of the central role these texts play in the life of Sri Lankan Buddhism, but these recitals are also popular in other Theravàda countries like Myanmar, Thailand, and Cambodia, and there is every reason to believe that their popularity is growing in those countries where the Buddhist community forms a small but significant minority like Bangladesh, Malaysia, and Indonesia, and in those Western countries where Buddhism has now taken root.  As it stands the Catubhāavārapāi is something of a misnomer, as there is an additional section added, not at the end of the four recitals, but right in the middle. This is the Atireka-Suttasattāni (the Seven Supplementary Discourses) beginning with the first discourse of the Buddha, the Dhammacakka-ppavattanasutta, Mahāsamayasutta from Dīghanikāya; followed by four                                                      1 In Sinhala the book is also known as the Piruvānā Pot Vahanse (The

Venerable Recitation Book); and the Mahā Pirit Pota (The Book of the Great Safeguards).

discourses from Suttanipāta mainly in verse; and ending with the Analysis of the Truths (Saccavibha!gasutta). Exactly when these discourses have been added to the original text is not clear, as all the evidence we have today includes this material.

The recitation has been further elaborated by the addition of the Mahāparitta (Great Protection) at the beginning of the recital, and the Avasànaü (Conclusion) at the end which, besides repeating some of the main discourses found in the Catubhāavārapāi (the Mahāma!gala-, Karaīyametta-, and Ratana-suttas), has other Canonical texts, not found in the main body of the work, like the Pa%iccasamuppāda, and the Pa%hama-

Buddhavacana, as well as texts written at some time in the Medieval period, like the Jayama!galagāthā, Aññhavãsatiparittaü, Jinapañjara and the Mahājayama!galagāthā, which is found both at the beginning and the end of the recital.   This book has been prepared in order to provide a reliable and complete text of the Catubhāavārapāi for those who would normally read or recite Pàëi through Latin script. The discourses and other material gathered in this book are not, and should not be regarded as, magical incantations. Verbally undertaking the Training Rules, without making an effort to maintain them unbroken is likely to be ineffective. Simply listening to a discourse about friendliness (mettā), without generating and radiating mettā, will similarly have little or no effect. Therefore in preparing this book every effort has been made to promote an intelligent participation in these recitals on the part of those who recite these texts, and those who listen to them. For that reason a line by line translation of the text has been adopted which should make it possible to follow the recital and the translation at the same time.  Those who are unable to attend a recital of these texts may still find much of interest in this collection, which includes the first discourse of the Buddha (Dhammacakkappavatanasutta), one of the most important discourses in the canon, together with an Analysis of the Truths (Saccavibha!gasutta), which was made by Venerable Sàriputta, one of the Buddha’s leading disciples. There are many discourses here that deal with various aspects of popular ethics, including the discourses on the Great Blessings (Mahāma!galasutta), the Advantages of Friendship (Mittānisasā), and the causes of Ruin (Parābhavasutta) among others. We may also mention here other pieces like the Reflections (Paccavekkhaā), which encourages frugality and contentment; and the recollection of the Thirty Two Parts of the body (Dvattisākāra), which is intended to counteract the lust, hatred, and delusion that arise in consequence of an over-attachment to the body and the pleasure that can be gained through that medium.  The two long discourses, Mahāsamayasutta & Ā%ānā%iyasutta, together with a number of shorter discourses in the first recital (see nos 13-16), should give the reader a fairly good outline of Buddhist cosmology. There are a number of discourses on mettā meditation, including the justly famous Karaīyamettasutta; and the Girimānandasutta outlines ten perceptions, or

contemplations, that can be undertaken by those who are intent on training the mind.  At the end of the book there is an appendix on the correct Pronunciation of Pàëi; and a short essay on the Prosody, which includes an outline of the metres that are used in the verse sections of the book, and which hopefully will help towards an appreciation of the aesthetic aspect of these texts.  Whenever these texts are recited let it be for the safety, peace, and happiness of all living beings. Having secured their lives on a firm foundation, may all beings then take steps to develop themselves further, until such time as they arrive at the complete cessation of suffering!      Dukkhappattā ca niddukkhā,     bhayappattā ca nibbhayā,     sokappattā ca nissokā -     hontu sabbe pi pā"ino!          (see pg. 277)

ânandajoti Bhikkhu Vassàna 2002/2546

About The Text

1: Authorities   The text of Catubhāavārapāi printed in the main section of this book has been prepared through a comparison of the following authorities, which are given here along with the abbreviations used in the variant readings. The texts and their recitation are as found in the Sinhalese tradition, note that there are sometimes different ways of reciting these texts in Burma and Thailand:

CBhp: Catubhāavārapāi, edited by Ven. Siri Sumanatissa Nàyaka Thero. Simon Hewavitarane Bequest Pàëi Text Series Vol VII. 1956, reprinted Colombo, 1992.  MPP: Maha Pirit Pota, edited by âcarya Sri Vàcissara Devundara Nàhimi, new edition by Makaladuve Sri Piyaratana Nàhimi. Colombo, 1995.  PPV: Piruvānā Pot Vahanse, edited by Attudàve Ràhula Sthavira. Taiwan, 1994.   PPV2: Piruvānā Pot Vahanse, edited by Bodàgama Candima Nàhimi. Taiwan, 2000.

The Commentary on Catubhāavārapāi, Sāratthasamuccaya, published in the Simon Hewavitarane Bequest Aññhakathà Series Vol XXVII, 1929 (reprinted 1992), was also consulted.

2: Variant Readings   There are some variations in the text which, as they make little difference in recital have not been noted in the variant readings, but which may usefully be

outlined here:1  CBhp, PPV2 sometimes print  for the more usual n, as in nibbāa, pahāa, sayahasamaya.  CBhp sometimes has  for l, as in antaikkha, piakā.  PPV2 sometimes has l for , as in Veluvane.  MPP sometimes prints n where we normally find , as in utuparināma, pisuna.  PPV, PPV2 quite often have  for ñ, and occasionally in place of other nasals, which is simply an alternative way of representing these sounds.

None of the books are entirely consistent in their usage, which may not be the fault of the editors, but because this is a collection of texts that was originally passed down in different manuscript traditions. However, in this edition I have preferred to prepare a text which is consistent, as far as that is possible.

Printer’s errors, like printing tī as the quotation marker, have also not

normally been noted, though where they amount to the omission of a word or line they have been included.

Owing to the Sinhala typeface used in MPP & PPV it is impossible to tell the difference between u & ū when in combination with certain letters, so that e.g. bhikkhu & bhikkhū are indistinguishable, except by context, and it was therefore not possible to note the variants in this regard.

1 Variant readings have only been recorded for the main part of the text (the

Catubhāavārapāi), and not for the Mahāparitta or Avasāna.

3: Comparison   Although Catubhāavārapāi is a collection of material drawn from the five Nikāyas, there are some significant differences between the suttas and other material in the collection and in the source. Below is a synopsis of where these works are originally found, together with a brief outline of the differences that are found (whenever they exist) for reference. It should be noted that variant readings are not mentioned here, but only major differences affecting either the title or contents:  1: Saraagamana (Mv I \[Vina. Mahàvaggo\]; Khp 1):

Mv I: no title; Khp: Saraattaya.

2: Dasasikkhāpadāni (Mv I \[Vin. Mahàvaggo\]; Khp 2): Mv I: no title; Khp: Dasasikkhāpada, also has the word samādiyāmi (I undertake) at the end of each precept.

3: Sāmaerapañha (Khp 4): Khp: Kumārapañha.

4: Dvattisākāra (Khp 3).

5: Paccavekkhaā (MN 2, passim): MN 2: no title.

6: Dasadhammasutta (AN 10:48): AN omits the nidāna (introduction) and the conclusion from Idam-avoca...onwards.

7: Mahāma!galasutta (Khp 5; Sn 2:8): Khp: Ma!galasutta.

8: Ratanasutta (Khp 6; Sn 2:1).

9: Karaīyamettasutta (Khp 9; Sn 1:8): Khp, Sn: Mettasutta.

10: Khandhaparitta (Cv V \[Vin. Cullavaggo\]; AN 4:67; cf. Jàtako 203): Cv V: no title, has different opening upto Na ha nūna..., replaces Idam-avoca Bhagavā, ida vatvā Sugato athāpara etad-avoca Satthā, with Evañ-ca pana bhikkhave kātabba AN omits Idam-avoca Bhagavā...Satthā. The verses recurr in the Jātako, but the prose there, although telling essentially the same story, is very different.

11: Mettānisasasutta (AN 11:16): AN omits nidāna, starts at Mettāya bhikkhave...; also omits Idam-avoca Bhagavā...to the end.

12: Mittānisasa (Jàtako 538): Jàtaka has no title.

13: Moraparitta (Jàtako 159): Jàtaka has no title.

14: Candaparitta (SN 2:9 \[Devaputtasayutta\]): SN has simply: Sāvatthiya viharati. Tena kho...etc.

15: Suriyaparitta (SN 2:10 \[Devaputtasayutta\]): SN omits the nidāna entirely, begins with Tena kho...

16: Dhajaggaparitta (SN 11:3 \[Sakkasayutta\]) SN has simply: Sāvatthiya viharati, followed by Bhūtapubba...etc.

17: Mahākassapattherabojjha!ga (SN 46:14 \[Bojjha!gasayutta\]): SN title: Gilāna 1.

18: Mahāmoggallānattherabojjha!ga (SN 46:15 \[Bojjha!gasayuttam\]): SN title: Gilāna 2.

19: Mahācundattherabojjha!ga (SN 46:16 \[Bojjha!gasayuttam\]):  SN title: Gilāna 3, omits the line: sāyanhasamaya patisallānā vu%%hito.

21: Isigilisutta (MN 116):

MN reads simply: Ari%%ho nāma bhikkhave Paccekabuddho, (as does PPV cf. variant readings).

Dhammacakkappavattanasutta (Mv I \[Vin. Mahāvaggo\]; SN 46:11 \[Saccasayutta\]): Sayutta has title as: Tathāgatena vutta 1 (but section title is Dhamma-cakkappavattanavaggo); abbreviates the list of devas by reading Brahmakāyikā devā instead of the full list. Mv I has no title, and also abbreviates the list of devas by reading Brahmakāyikā devā instead of the full list.

23: Mahāsamayasutta (DN 20).

24: Ālavakasutta (SN 10:12 \[Yakkhasayuttam\]; Sn 1:10): SN has the title Ālavi; omits the line beginning Atha kho... before the verses, includes an extra line Asmā lokā para loka ~ eva pecca na socati at end of verse 7; omits the prose found after the verse.

25: Kasībhāradvājasutta (SN 7:11 \[Brāhmaasayuttam\]; Sn 1:4): Sn is the same as here. SN, however, has the title as Kasi; omits the first 3 prose lines after verse 5, replaces Kasãbhàradvàja’s request for ordination, and subsequent attainment with a request to be accepted as a lay disciple.

26: Parābhavasutta (Sn 1:6).

27: Vasalasutta (Sn 1:7).

28: Saccavibha!gasutta (MN 141).

29: Ā%ānā%iyasutta, pt 1 (DN 32): DN has the title as -suttanta.

4: Layout & Punctuation

a) In prose lines the text and translation normally start parallel to each other at the side of the page, e.g. from the Dasasikkhāpadāni:  Pā"ātipātā verama"īsikkhāpada. The training rule of refraining from killing living creatures.  b) Some prose lines have been indented for emphasis, e.g.

Idam-avoca Bhagavā, The Auspicious One said this,

c) Some prose lines have been centred, example from Saraagamana:

Buddha sara"a gacchāmi I go to the Buddha for refuge

d) In verse lines the Pàëi is indented in relation to the translation, and each metre is distinguished by the layout (for the details on this see the 2nd appendix on prosody) example from Mahāma!galasutta:

1. Bahū devā manussā ca ~ ma=galāni acintayu Many are the gods and men ~ who have thought about the blessings  e) Owing to the different grammatical structure of the languages it has occasionally been necessary to take two lines of Pàëi together for the purposes of translation, this is indicated by the sign ˚ appearing at the beginning of a line of translation.  f) In the Siloka verses when the two parts of the line have been taken together for translation, this is indicated by the omission of the wavy slash otherwise dividing the line in the translation. Example from Mahāsamayasutta:  10.  Sata eke sahassāna ~ amanussānam-addasu,  Some of them saw a hundred thousand of those non-human beings,    appekenantam-addakkhu ~ disā sabbā phuā ahū. and some saw an endless number ~ spread out in every direction.

Note to the 2nd Edition

After the publication of the 1st edition a number of minor mistakes were brought to my notice, chiefly through the careful reading of the text made by Mr. Kariyawasam, late editor at B.P.S., and corrections have been included in the 2nd edition.

Note on the Mahāparitta & Avasāna

The Mahāparitta and Avasāna as presented here contain all the material necessary for a complete recital. Sometimes, however, according to different traditions or

circumstances, material may be added to - or omitted from - the texts as given here.

Note that the Jayama!galagāthā is recited only on

auspicious (ma!gala) occasions, and never on (avama!gala) inauspicious ones.

Mahàparittaü

Paiccasamuppāda

Conditional Arising  Avijjāpaccayā sa=khārā, Because of ignorance there are (volitional) processes,  sa=khārapaccayā viññā"a, because of (volitional) processes: consciousness,  viññā"apaccayā nāmarūpa, because of consciousness: name-and-form,  nāmarūpapaccayā saEāyatana, because of name-and-form: the six sense spheres,  saEāyatanapaccayā phasso, because of the six sense spheres: contact,  phassapaccayā vedanā, because of contact: feeling,  vedanāpaccayā ta"hā, because of feeling: craving,  ta"hāpaccayā upādāna, because of craving: attachment,  upādānapaccayā bhavo, because of attachment: continuation,  bhavapaccayā jāti, because of continuation: birth,  jātipaccayā jarāmara"a, because of birth: old age, death,  sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti, grief, lamentation, pain, sorrow, and despair (all) originate,  evam-etassa kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa samudayo hoti. and so this whole mass of suffering comes to arise.

Mahàparittaü

Avijjāya tveva asesavirāganirodhā sa=khāranirodho, But with the complete fading away and cessation of ignorance, there is the cessation of (volitional) processes,  sa=khāranirodhā viññā"anirodho, with the cessation of (volitional) processes, the cessation of consciousness,  viññā"anirodhā nāmarūpanirodho, with the cessation of consciousness, the cessation of name-and-form,  nāmarūpanirodhā saEāyatananirodho, with the cessation of name-and-form, the cessation of the six sense  spheres,  saEāyatananirodhā phassanirodho, with the cessation of the six sense spheres, the cessation of contact,  phassanirodhā vedanānirodho, with the cessation of contact, the cessation of feeling,  vedanānirodhā ta"hānirodho, with the cessation of feeling, the cessation of craving,  ta"hānirodhā upādānanirodho, with the cessation of craving, the cessation of attachment,  upādānanirodhā bhavanirodho, with the cessation of attachment, the cessation of continuation,  bhavanirodhā jātinirodho, with the cessation of continuation, the cessation of birth,  jātinirodhā jarāmara"a with the cessation of birth, old age, death,  sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā nirujjhanti, grief, lamentation, pain, sorrow, and despair (all) cease,  evam-etassa kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa nirodho hoti. and so there is a cessation of this whole mass of suffering.

Verses on the Blessings of Success  1.  Bāhu sahassam-abhinimmitasāyudha ta, ˚ Màra, the terrible one, who had created a thousand arms with weapons,    Girimekhala uditaghorasasenamāra, and had mounted on (the elephant) Girimekhala, with his army,    dānādidhammavidhinā jitavā munindo - did the lord of sages defeat by such things as generosity and so forth -    ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   2.  Mārātirekam-abhiyujjhita sabbaratti, ˚ Even more terrible than Màra was the impatient and obdurate yakkha âlavaka,    ghora pan’ Ālavakam-akkhamathaddhayakkha, who fought (with the Buddha) throughout the whole night,    khantisudantavidhinā jitavā munindo - (him) did the lord of sages defeat by his patience and self-control    ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!

Mahàparittaü

3. Nālāgiri gajavara atimattabhūta, The great elephant Nàlàgiri, who was completely intoxicated,    dāvaggicakkam-asanīva sudāru"a ta, fearful, like a jungle fire, a flaming discus, or a flash of lightning,    mettambusekavidhinā jitavā munindo - did the lord of sages defeat by sprinkling (him) with the waters of friendliness -    ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   4.  Ukkhittakhaggam-atihatthasudāru"a ta With a sword that was lifted high in his outstretched hand, that very fearsome (bandit)    dhāva tiyojanapatha=gulimālavanta: the one with a finger necklace (Aïgulimàla) ran for (the whole of) three leagues up the path:    iddhībhisa=khatamano jitavā munindo - (him) did the lord of sages defeat with the psychic powers created in his mind -     ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   5.  Katvāna kaham-udara iva gabbhinīyā, Having made her midriff (look large) with a piece of wood, like one who is pregnant,    Ciñcāya duhavacana janakāyamajjhe: Ciñcà (then spoke) wicked words in the midst of a group of people:    santena somavidhinā jitavā munindo - (her) did the lord of sages defeat by means of his gentleness and peacefulness -

Mahàparittaü

ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   6.  Sacca vihāya mati-Saccakavādaketu, The arrogant Saccaka, who had given up the truth, and had challenged (the Buddha) to a debate,    vādābhiropitamana ati-andhabhūta, with his mind fixed on his argument, completely blind to the truth,    paññāpadīpajalito jitavā munindo - (him) did the lord of sages defeat with the shining lamp of true wisdom -     ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   7.  Nandopanandabhujaga vibudha mahiddhi, ˚ Taming the wise dragon Nandopananda, who had great power,    puttena therabhujagena damāpayanto, by way of his son (Moggallàna), who was a dragon of an elder,    iddhūpadesavidhinā jitavā munindo - (thus) did the lord of sages defeat (him) by instructing (his son to use) psychic power -    ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   8.  Duggāhadihibhujagena sudahahattha, He whose hand was badly bitten by the serpent of wrongly taken up views,    brahma visuddhijutim-iddhi-Bakābhidhāna, the brahma Baka, who was clear and bright, and who possessed psychic powers,    ñā"āgadena vidhinā jitavā munindo - did the lord of sages defeat with the medicine of his super-knowledge -

Mahàparittaü

ta tejasā bhavatu te jayama=galāni! through that resplendent power may you have the blessings of success!   9.  Etā pi Buddhajayama=gala-ahagāthā, ˚ He who recites these eight verses each and every day,    yo vācako dinadine sarate-m-atandī, which are about the Buddha’s (many) blesséd successes, and remembers them with diligence,    hitvānanekavividhāni cupaddavāni, after giving up (all) the many and various kinds of adversity,    mokkha sukha adhigameyya naro sapañño. that wise man will (soon thereafter) attain both freedom and happiness.

Mahāma=galasutta

The Discourse on the Great Blessings

#### Eva me suta: Thus I have heard:

eka samaya Bhagavā Sāvatthiya viharati at one time the Auspicious One was dwelling near Sàvatthi  Jetavane Anāthapi"Jikassa ārāme. at Anàthapiõóika’s grounds in Jeta’s Wood.  Atha kho aññatarā devatā abhikkantāya rattiyā, Then a certain god, towards the end of the night,  abhikkantava""ā kevalakappa Jetavana obhāsetvā, having lit up the whole of Jeta’s Wood with his surpassing beauty,  yena Bhagavā tenupasa=kami,  approached the Auspicious One,

Mahàparittaü

upasa=kamitvā Bhagavanta abhivādetvā, ekam-anta ahāsi. and after approaching and worshipping the Auspicious One, he stood on one side.  Ekam-anta hitā kho sā devatā Bhagavanta gāthāya ajjhabhāsi: While standing on one side that god addressed the Auspicious One with a verse:   1.  “Bahū devā manussā ca ~ ma=galāni acintayu “Many are the gods and the men ~ who have thought about the blessings    āka=khamānā sotthāna: ~ brūhi ma=galam-uttama.” hoping for safety: ~ now please say what is the supreme blessing.”   2.  “Asevanā ca bālāna, ~ pa"Jitānañ-ca sevanā, “Not associating with fools, ~ but associating with the wise,    pūjā ca pūjanīyāna: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. honouring those worthy of honour: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   3.  Pairūpadesavāso ca, ~ pubbe ca katapuññatā,  Living in a suitable place, ~ formerly having done good deeds,    attasammāpa"idhi ca: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. having the right aspiration for oneself: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   4.  Bāhusaccañ-ca sippañ-ca, ~ vinayo ca susikkhito, Having great learning and craft, ~ being disciplined and well trained,    subhāsitā ca yā vācā: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. and whatever words are well spoken: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   5.  Mātāpitu-upahāna, ~ puttadārassa sa=gaho, Attending on one’s mother and father, ~ looking after one’s wife and children,    anākulā ca kammantā: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. having work that is not agitating: ~ this is the supreme blessing.

Mahàparittaü

6. Dānañ-ca Dhammacariyā ca, ~ ñātakānañ-ca sa=gaho, Giving, and living by the Dhamma, ~ and looking after one’s relatives,    anavajjāni kammāni: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. (performing) actions that are blameless: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   7.   Ārati virati pāpā, ~ majjapānā ca saññamo, Abstaining, refraining, from bad deeds, ~ restraint from intoxicating drink,    appamādo ca dhammesu: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. being heedful regarding (all) things: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   8.   Gāravo ca nivāto ca, ~ santuhī ca kataññutā, Having respect and being humble, ~ being satisfied and grateful,    kālena Dhammasava"a: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. listening to the Dhamma at the right time: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   9.   Khantī ca sovacassatā, ~ sama"ānañ-ca dassana, Being patient and easily spoken to, ~ seeing ascetics,    kālena Dhammasākacchā: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. discussing the Dhamma at the right time: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   10. Tapo ca brahmacariyañ-ca, ~ ariyasaccānadassana, Austerity, living spiritually, ~ insight into the noble truths,    nibbānasacchikiriyā ca: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. the experience of Nibbàna: ~ this is the supreme blessing.   11.  Phuhassa lokadhammehi, ~ citta yassa na kampati, He whose mind does not waver, when it is touched by wordly things,    asoka viraja khema: ~ eta ma=galam-uttama. being griefless, dustless, and secure: ~ this is the supreme blessing.

Mahàparittaü

12. Etādisāni katvāna, ~ sabbattha-m-aparājitā, Having done as here directed, ~ being undefeated everywhere,    sabbattha sotthi gacchanti: ~ ta tesa ma=galam-uttaman”-ti. they go everywhere in safety: ~ for them this is the supreme blessing.”

Verse of Blessing    Etena saccavajjena ~ sotthi te hotu sabbadā! By this declaration of the truth ~ may you be safe at all times!    Etena saccavajjena ~ hotu te jayama=gala! By this declaration of the truth ~ may you have the blessing of success!    Etena saccavajjena ~ sabbarogo vinassatu! By this declaration of the truth ~ may all disease be destroyed!

Kara"īyamettasutta

#### The Discourse on how Friendliness Meditation should be Done

1. Kara"īyam-atthakusalena,      What should be done by one skilled in goodness,       yan-ta santa pada abhisamecca:     who has comprehended the state of peace:        sakko ujū ca sūjū ca,      ˚ he ought to be able, straight, and upright,       suvaco cassa mudu anatimānī,     easy to speak to, meek, without conceit,  2.       santussako ca subharo ca,      satisfied (with little), easy to support,       appakicco ca sallahukavutti,      free from duties, and light in living,

Mahàparittaü

santindriyo ca nipako ca,      with faculties at peace, prudent,       appagabbho kulesu ananugiddho,     not forward, and greedless among the families,  3.       na ca khudda samācare kiñci      he should not do the slightest thing       yena viññū pare upavadeyyu.     whereby others who are wise might find fault (with him).        “Sukhino vā khemino hontu,      “(May all beings) be happy and secure,       sabbe sattā bhavantu sukhitattā!     may all beings in their hearts be happy!  4.       Ye keci pā"abhūtatthi -      Whatsoever breathing beings there are -       tasā vā thāvarā vā anavasesā,     trembling, firm, or any other (beings),        dīghā vā ye mahantā vā,      whether they be long or great,       majjhimā rassakā"ukathūlā,     of middle size, short, tiny, or of compact (body),  5.       dihā vā ye ca addihā,      those who are seen, and those who are unseen,       ye ca dūre vasanti avidūre,     those who live far away, those who are near,        bhūtā vā sambhavesī vā -      those who are born, and those who still seek birth -       sabbe sattā bhavantu sukhitattā!”     may all beings in their hearts be happy!”

Mahàparittaü

6. Na paro para nikubbetha,      No one should cheat another,       nātimaññetha katthaci na kañci,     nor should he despise anyone wherever he is,        byārosanā paighasaññā      ˚ he should not long for suffering for another       nāññam-aññassa dukkham-iccheyya.     because of anger or resentment.  7.       Mātā yathā niya putta      ˚ In the same way as a mother would protect       āyusā ekaputtam-anurakkhe,     her child, her only child, with her life,        evam-pi sabbabhūtesu      so too towards all beings       mānasa bhāvaye aparimā"a.     one should develop the measureless thought (of friendliness).  8.       Mettañ-ca sabbalokasmi      ˚ Towards the entire world he should develop       mānasa bhāvaye aparimā"a,     the measureless thought of friendliness,        uddha adho ca tiriyañ-ca,      above, below, and across (the middle),       asambādha avera asapatta.     without barriers, hate, or enmity.  9.       Tiha cara nisinno vā,      Standing, walking, sitting,       sayāno vā yāvatassa vigatamiddho,     lying, for as long as he is without torpor,

Mahàparittaü

eta sati adhiheyya,      he should be resolved on this mindfulness,       brahmam-eta vihāra idha-m-āhu.     for this, they say here, is the (true) spiritual life.  10.      Dihiñ-ca anupagamma,       Without going near to (wrong) views,       sīlavā dassanena sampanno,      virtuous, and endowed with (true) insight,        kāmesu vineyya gedha,      having removed (all) greed for sense pleasures,       na hi jātu gabbhaseyya punar-etī ti.     he will never come to lie in a womb again.

Ratanasutta

The Discourse on the Treasures  1.     Yānīdha bhūtāni samāgatāni,     Whatever beings have come together here,       Bhummāni vā yāni va antalikkhe,     whether of the earth or in the firmament,       sabbe va bhūtā sumanā bhavantu,     may the minds of all those beings be happy,       atho pi sakkacca su"antu bhāsita.     and may they listen carefully to what is said.   2.      Tasmā hi bhūtā nisāmetha sabbe,     Therefore, all of you beings, be attentive,        metta karotha mānusiyā pajāya,     be friendly towards this generation of men,          divā ca ratto ca haranti ye bali,     they who bring offerings by day and by night,        tasmā hi ne rakkhatha appamattā.      therefore, being heedful, you must protect them.  3.     Ya kiñci vitta - idha vā hura vā     Whatever riches there are - here or hereafter       saggesu vā - ya ratana pa"īta     or in the heavens - that excellent treasure       na no sama atthi Tathāgatena -     is not equal unto the Realised One -       idam-pi Buddhe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Buddha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!

Mahàparittaü

4. Khaya virāga amata pa"īta -     (Craving’s) end, dispassion, deathlessness, excellence -       yad-ajjhagā Sakyamunī samāhito -     that which the concentrated Sakyan sage attained -       na tena Dhammena samatthi kiñci -     there is nothing that is equal to that state -       idam-pi Dhamme ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Dhamma:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   5.      Yam-Buddhaseho pariva""ayī suci -     That which the great Buddha praised as being pure -       samādhim-ānantarikañ-ñam-āhu -     the concentration said to have immediate (result) -       samādhinā tena samo na vijjati -     no equal to that concentration is found -       idam-pi Dhamme ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Dhamma       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   6.     Ye puggalā aha sata pasatthā -     Those eight individuals praised by the good -       cattāri etāni yugāni honti -     there are these four pairs (of persons) -       te dakkhi"eyyā Sugatassa sāvakā,     those disciples of the Fortunate One are worthy of gifts,

Mahàparittaü

etesu dinnāni mahapphalāni -     those things that have been given to them have great fruit -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   7.      Ye suppayuttā manasā daEhena     Those who have firm minds that are devoted to       nikkāmino Gotamasāsanamhi -     Gotama’s teaching, being free from sense desire -       te pattipattā amata vigayha -     having attained they entered the deathless -       laddhā mudhā nibbuti bhuñjamānā -     are enjoying emancipation, obtained for free -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   8.      Yathindakhīlo pahavi sito siyā     Just as a locking post stuck fast in the earth       catubbhi vātehi asampakampiyo,     does not waver on account of the four winds,       tathūpama sappurisa vadāmi,     just like this, I say, is the person who is true,       yo ariyasaccāni avecca passati -     the one who sees the noble truths completely -

Mahàparittaü

idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   9.      Ye ariyasaccāni vibhāvayanti,     Those who clearly distinguish the noble truths,       gambhīrapaññena sudesitāni,     which have been well preached by the one with great wisdom,       kiñcāpi te honti bhusappamattā     no matter how great they become in heedlessness       na te bhava ahama ādiyanti -     still they do not take up an eighth existence -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:        etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   10.     Sahā vassa dassanasampadāya      With his attainment of (liberating) insight       tayas su dhammā jahitā bhavanti:     there are three things that are given up:       sakkāyadihi vicikicchitañ-ca     embodiment view, uncertainty, and       sīlabbata vā pi yad-atthi kiñci.     whatever (grasping at) virtue and practices there is.       Catūhapāyehi ca vippamutto,     He is free from (rebirth in) the four lower worlds,

Mahàparittaü

cha cābhihānāni abhabbo kātu -     and he is incapable of the six great crimes -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   11.     Kiñcāpi so kamma karoti pāpaka     Whatever bad actions he performs       kāyena vācā uda cetasā vā,     by way of body, speech, or mind,       abhabbo so tassa paicchādāya:     he is incapable of covering it up:       abhabbatā dihapadassa vuttā -     this incapacity is said of one who has seen the state (of peace) -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!     12.    Vanappagumbe yathā phussitagge     Just like a tall woodland tree crowned with flowers       gimhānamāse pahamasmi gimhe,     in the summer months, in the early summer,       tathūpama Dhammavara adesayī,     just like this he preached the Dhamma which is best,       Nibbānagāmi paramahitāya -     which goes to Nibbàna, the highest benefit -

Mahàparittaü

idam-pi Buddhe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Buddha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   13.     Varo varaññū varado varāharo,     The best one, knowing the best, giving the best, brought the best,       anuttaro Dhammavara adesayī -     the unsurpassed one preached the Dhamma which is best -       idam-pi Buddhe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Buddha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   14.     Khī"a purā"a nava natthi sambhava,     The old is destroyed, and nothing new is produced,       virattacittā āyatike bhavasmi,     their minds are unexcited by future rebirth,       te khī"abījā avirūEhicchandā,     they have destroyed the seeds, and have no desire for growth,       nibbanti dhīrā yathāyam-padīpo -     the wise are still, just as this lamp (is still) -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!

(Spoken by Sakka, lord of the gods:)  15.     Yānīdha bhūtāni samāgatāni,     Whatever beings have come together here,       bhummāni vā yāni va antalikkhe,     whether of the earth or in the firmament,        Tathāgata devamanussapūjita,     gods and men revere the Realised One,       Buddha namassāma - suvatthi hotu!     we honour the Buddha - may there be safety!   16. The Verses on the Great Blessings of Success   1.   Mahākāru"iko nātho, ~ hitāya sabbapā"ina, The lord of great compassion, ~ for the benefit of all living creatures,    pūretvā pāramī sabbā ~ patto Sambodhim-uttama. having fulfilled all the perfections ~ has attained supreme and Complete Awakening.    Etena saccavajjena ~ hotu te jayama=gala! By this declaration of the truth ~ may you have the blessing of success!   2.   Jayanto Bodhiyā mūle ~ Sakyāna nandivaddhano - Succeeding at the root of the Bodhi tree ~ he furthered the Sakyans’ joy -    eva tuyha jayo hotu, ~ jayassu jayama=gala! so may you be successful, ~ may you succeed with the blessing of success!   3.   Sakkatvā Buddharatana, ~ osadha uttama vara, Having honoured the Buddha treasure, ~ the best and supreme medicine,    hita devamanussāna, ~ Buddhatejena sotthinā of benefit to gods and men, ~ by the blesséd power of the Buddha

Mahàparittaü

nassantupaddavā sabbe, ~ dukkhā vūpasamentu te! may all adversities perish, ~ and all suffering come to an end for you!   4.   Sakkatvā Dhammaratana, ~ osadha uttama vara, Having honoured the Dhamma treasure, ~ the best and supreme medicine,    pariEāhūpasamana, ~ Dhammatejena sotthinā which brings fevers to an end, ~ by the blesséd power of the Dhamma    nassantupaddavā sabbe, ~ bhayā vūpasamentu te! may all adversities perish, ~ and all fear come to an end for you!   5.   Sakkatvā Sa=gharatana, ~ osadha uttama vara, Having honoured the Saïgha treasure, ~ the best and supreme medicine,    āhuneyya pāhuneyya, ~ Sa=ghatejena sotthinā worthy of gifts and hospitality, ~ by the blesséd power of the Saïgha    nassantupaddavā sabbe, ~ rogā vūpasamentu te! may all adversities perish, ~ and all disease come to an end for you!   6. 3: Sāma"erapañha1

The Questions to the Novice

Eka nāma ki?  What is said to be one?

Sabbe sattā āhārahitikā.  All beings subsist on food.

Dve nāma ki?  What is said to be two?

Nāmañ-ca rūpañ-ca.  Name-and-form.

Tī"i nāma ki?  What is said to be three?

Tisso vedanā.  The three feelings.

Cattāri nāma ki?  What is said to be four?

Cattāri ariyasaccāni.  The four noble truths.

Pañca nāma ki?  What is said to be five?

Pañcupādānakkhandhā.2  The five constituent groups (of mind and body) that are attached to.

Cha nāma ki?  What is said to be six?

Cha ajjhattikāni āyatanāni.  The six internal sense spheres.

Satta nāma ki?  What is said to be seven?

Satta Bojjha=gā.  The seven factors of Awakening.

Aha nāma ki?  What is said to be eight?

Ariyo ahangiko maggo.  The noble path with eight factors.

Nava nāma ki?  What is said to be nine?

Nava sattāvāsā.  The nine abodes of beings.

Dasa nāma ki?  What is said to be ten?

Dasaha=gehi samannāgato Arahā ti vuccatī ti.  When endowed with ten factors he is said to be Worthy.

8. Ratanasutta

The Discourse on the Treasures  Introductory Verses    Koisatasahassesu ~ cakkavāEesu devatā The gods in the ten hundred thousand million universes    yassā"a patiga"hanti ~ yañ-ca Vesāliya pure, accepted that order (given) in the city of Vesàli,    rogāmanusssadubbhikkha ~ - sambhūta tividha bhaya - and disease, non-human beings, and famine ~ - the threefold fear that had arisen -    khippam-antaradhāpesi, ~ paritta ta bha"āmahe. was quickly brought to an end, ~ we will recite that safeguard.  The Safeguard  1.     Yānīdha bhūtāni samāgatāni,     Whatever beings have come together here,       Bhummāni vā yāni va antalikkhe,     whether of the earth or in the firmament,

Pañhamakabhàõavàraü

sabbe va bhūtā sumanā bhavantu,     may the minds of all those beings be happy,       atho pi sakkacca su"antu bhāsita.     and may they listen carefully to what is said.   2.      Tasmā hi bhūtā nisāmetha sabbe,     Therefore, all of you beings, be attentive,        metta karotha mānusiyā pajāya,     be friendly towards this generation of men,          divā ca ratto ca haranti ye bali,     they who bring offerings by day and by night,        tasmā hi ne rakkhatha appamattā.      therefore, being heedful, you must protect them.  3.     Ya kiñci vitta - idha vā hura vā     Whatever riches there are - here or hereafter       saggesu vā - ya ratana pa"īta     or in the heavens - that excellent treasure       na no sama atthi Tathāgatena -     is not equal unto the Realised One -       idam-pi Buddhe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Buddha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   4.     Khaya virāga amata pa"īta -     (Craving’s) end, dispassion, deathlessness, excellence -       yad-ajjhagā Sakyamunī samāhito -     that which the concentrated Sakyan sage attained -       na tena Dhammena samatthi kiñci -     there is nothing that is equal to that state -

Pañhamakabhàõavàraü

idam-pi Dhamme ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Dhamma:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!  5.      Yam-Buddhaseho pariva""ayī suci -     That which the great Buddha praised as being pure -       samādhim-ānantarikañ-ñam-āhu -     the concentration said to have immediate (result) -       samādhinā tena samo na vijjati -     no equal to that concentration is found -       idam-pi Dhamme ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Dhamma       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   6.     Ye puggalā aha sata pasatthā -     Those eight individuals praised by the good -       cattāri etāni yugāni honti -     there are these four pairs (of persons) -       te dakkhi"eyyā Sugatassa sāvakā,     those disciples of the Fortunate One are worthy of gifts,       etesu dinnāni mahapphalāni -     those things that have been given to them have great fruit -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!

Pañhamakabhàõavàraü

7. Ye suppayuttā manasā daEhena     Those who have firm minds that are devoted to       nikkāmino Gotamasāsanamhi -     Gotama’s teaching, being free from sense desire -       te pattipattā amata vigayha -     having attained they entered the deathless -       laddhā mudhā nibbuti bhuñjamānā -     are enjoying emancipation, obtained for free -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   8.      Yathindakhīlo pahavi sito siyā     Just as a locking post stuck fast in the earth       catubbhi vātehi asampakampiyo,     does not waver on account of the four winds,       tathūpama sappurisa vadāmi,     just like this, I say, is the person who is true,       yo ariyasaccāni avecca passati -     the one who sees the noble truths completely -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!

Pañhamakabhàõavàraü

9. Ye ariyasaccāni vibhāvayanti,     Those who clearly distinguish the noble truths,       gambhīrapaññena sudesitāni,     which have been well preached by the one with great wisdom,       kiñcāpi te honti bhusappamattā     no matter how great they become in heedlessness       na te bhava ahama ādiyanti -     still they do not take up an eighth existence -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:        etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!   10.     Sahā vassa dassanasampadāya      With his attainment of (liberating) insight       tayas su dhammā jahitā bhavanti:     there are three things that are given up:       sakkāyadihi vicikicchitañ-ca     embodiment view, uncertainty, and       sīlabbata vā pi yad-atthi kiñci.     whatever (grasping at) virtue and practices there is.       Catūhapāyehi ca vippamutto,     He is free from (rebirth in) the four lower worlds,       cha cābhihānāni abhabbo kātu -     and he is incapable of the six great crimes -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!

Pañhamakabhàõavàraü

11. Kiñcāpi so kamma karoti pāpaka     Whatever bad actions he performs       kāyena vācā uda cetasā vā,     by way of body, speech, or mind,       abhabbo so tassa paicchādāya:     he is incapable of covering it up:       abhabbatā dihapadassa vuttā -     this incapacity is said of one who has seen the state (of peace) -       idam-pi Sa=ghe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Saïgha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!     12.     Vanappagumbe yathā phussitagge     Just like a tall woodland tree crowned with flowers       gimhānamāse pahamasmi gimhe,     in the summer months, in the early summer,       tathūpama Dhammavara adesayī,     just like this he preached the Dhamma which is best,       Nibbānagāmi paramahitāya -     which goes to Nibbàna, the highest benefit -       idam-pi Buddhe ratana pa"īta:     this excellent treasure is in the Buddha:       etena saccena suvatthi hotu!     by virtue of this truth may there be safety!

Katamā dasa? What are the ten?

1 CBhp: ābādhiko hoti

Dutiyakabhàõavàraü

i.  Aniccasaññā,    The perception of impermanence,  ii.  anattasaññā,    the perception of non-self,  iii. asubhasaññā,    the perception of the unattractive,  iv. ādīnavasaññā,    the perception of danger,  v.  pahānasaññā,    the perception of giving up,  vi. virāgasaññā,    the perception of dispassion,  vii. nirodhasaññā,    the perception of cessation,  viii. sabbaloke anabhiratasaññā,    the perception of non-delight in the whole world,  ix. sabbasa=khāresu aniccasaññā,    the perception of impermanence in all processes,  x.  ānāpānasati.   mindfulness while breathing.

- \*     \*   i.  Katamā c’ Ānanda aniccasaññā?

Now what, ânanda, is the perception of impermanence?  Idh’ Ānanda bhikkhu araññagato vā, rukkhamūlagato vā,  Here, ânanda, a monk who has gone to the wilderness, or to the root of a tree,

Dutiyakabhàõavàraü

suññāgāragato vā, iti paisañcikkhati:  or to an empty place, considers thus:

rūpa anicca  form is impermanent  vedanā aniccā  feelings are impermanent  saññā aniccā  perceptions are impermanent  sa=khārā aniccā (mental) processes are impermanent  viññā"a aniccan-ti.  consciousness is impermanent.

Iti imesu pañcasupādānakkhandhesu aniccānupassī viharati. Thus in regard to these five constituent groups (of mind and body) that are attached to he dwells contemplating impermanence.  Aya vuccat’ Ānanda aniccasaññā.  This, ânanda, is called the perception of impermanence.    ii.  Katamā c’ Ānanda anattasaññā?

Now what, ânanda, is the perception of non-self?  Idh’ Ānanda bhikkhu araññagato vā, rukkhamūlagato vā,  Here, ânanda, a monk who has gone to the wilderness, or to the root of a tree,  suññāgāragato vā, iti paisañcikkhati:  or to an empty place, considers thus:

Cakkhu anattā - rūpā1 anattā  the eye is not self - forms are not self

1 CBhp, MPP, PPV2: rūpa

Dutiyakabhàõavàraü

sota anattā - saddā anattā the ear is not self - sounds are not self  ghā"a anattā - gandhā anattā the nose is not self - smells are not self  jivhā anattā - rasā anattā the tongue is not self - tastes are not self  kāyo anattā - phohabbā anattā the body is not self - tangibles are not self  mano anattā - dhammā anattā ti.  the mind is not self - thoughts are not self.

Iti imesu chasu ajjhattikabāhiresu āyatanesu  Thus in regard to these six internal and external sense spheres  anattānupassī viharati.  he dwells contemplating non-self.  Aya vuccat’ Ānanda anattasaññā.  This, ânanda, is called the perception of non-self.   iii. Katamā c’ Ānanda asubhasaññā?

Now what, ânanda, is the perception of the unattractive?  Idh’ Ānanda bhikkhu imam-eva kāya -  Here, ânanda, a monk (in regard to) this body -  uddha pādatalā, adho kesamatthakā, tacapariyanta, from the sole of the feet upwards, from the hair of the head down, bounded by the skin,   pūra nānappakārassa asucino - paccavekkhati: and filled with manifold impurities - reflects (thus):

kesā, lomā, nakhā, dantā, taco, hairs of the head, body hairs, nails, teeth, skin,  masa, nahāru, ahi, ahimiñjā,1 vakka,  flesh, sinews, bones, bone-marrow, kidney,  hadaya, yakana, kilomaka, pihaka, papphāsa,  heart, liver, pleura, spleen, lungs,  anta, antagu"a, udariya, karīsa,  intestines, mesentery, undigested food, excrement,  pitta, semha, pubbo, lohita, sedo, medo, bile, phlegm, pus, blood, sweat, fat,  assu, vasā, kheEo, si=ghānikā, lasikā, muttan-ti.  tears, grease, spit, mucus, synovic fluid, urine.

Iti imasmi kāye asubhānupassī viharati. Thus in regard to this body he dwells contemplating what is unattractive.  Aya vuccat’ Ānanda asubhasaññā.  This, ânanda, is called the perception of the unattractive.   iv. Katamā c’ Ānanda ādīnavasaññā?

Now what, ânanda, is the perception of danger?  Idh’ Ānanda bhikkhu araññagato vā, rukkhamūlagato vā,  Here, ânanda, a monk who has gone to the wilderness, or to the root of a tree,  suññāgāragato vā, iti paisañcikkhati:  or to an empty place, considers thus:  Bahu dukkho kho aya kāyo bahu ādīnavo, This body has many sufferings, many dangers,

cakkhurogo, sotarogo, ghā"arogo, jivhārogo, kāyarogo,  eye-disease, ear-disease, nose-disease, tongue-disease, body-disease (i.e diseases affecting the sense spheres),  sīsarogo, ka""arogo, mukharogo, dantarogo,  head-disease, ear-disease, mouth-disease, tooth-disease,  kāso, sāso, pināso, Jaho, jaro, cough, asthma, catarrh, pyrexia, fever,   kucchirogo, mucchā, pakkhandikā,1 sūlā, visūcikā, stomach-ache, fainting, diarrhoea, gripes, cholera,   kuha, ga"Jo, kilāso, soso, apamāro,  leprosy, boils, eczema, consumption, epilepsy,  daddu, ka"Ju, kacchu, rakhasā,2 vitacchikā, ringworm, itch, scab, chickenpox, scabies,  lohitapitta, madhumeho, asā, piEakā, bhagandalā, haemorrhage, diabetes, piles, cancer, ulcers,   pittasamuhānā ābādhā, semhasamuhānā ābādhā,  afflictions arising from excess bile, afflictions arising from excess phlegm,  vātasamuhānā ābādhā, sannipātikā ābādhā,  afflictions arising from excess wind, afflictions arising from a conflict of humours,  utupari"āmajā ābādhā, visamaparihārajā ābādhā,3 afflictions born of a change of season, afflictions born of not being careful,

opakkamikā ābādhā, kammavipākajā ābādhā,  afflictions from being attacked, afflictions born as a result of (previous unwholesome) actions,  sīta, u"ha, jighacchā, pipāsā, uccāro, passāvo ti.  cold, heat, hunger, thirst, stool, urine.

Iti imasmi kāye ādīnavānupassī viharati.  Thus, in regard to this body, he dwells contemplating danger.  Aya vuccat’ Ānanda ādīnavasaññā.  This, ânanda, is called the perception of danger.   v.  Katamā c’ Ānanda pahānasaññā?

Now what, ânanda, is the perception of giving up?  Idh’ Ānanda bhikkhu uppanna kāmavitakka nādhivāseti, Here, ânanda, a monk does not consent to thoughts of sense desire that have arisen,  pajahati, vinodeti, byantīkaroti, anabhāva gameti. (these) he gives up, dispels, brings to an end, and makes non-existent.   Uppannuppanne pāpake akusale dhamme nādhivāseti, He does not consent to any bad, unwholesome, thoughts that have arisen,

pajahati, vinodeti, byantīkaroti, anabhāva gameti.  (these) he gives up, dispels, brings to an end, and makes non-existent.  Aya vuccat’ Ānanda pahānasaññā. This, ânanda, is called the perception of giving up.    vi. Katamā c’ Ānanda virāgasaññā?

Now what, ânanda, is the perception of dispassion?  Idh’ Ānanda bhikkhu araññagato vā, rukkhamūlagato vā,  Here, ânanda, a monk who has gone to the wilderness, or to the root of a  tree,  suññāgāragato vā, iti paisañcikkhati:  or to an empty place, considers thus:    Eta santa, eta pa"īta,

ye loke upāyupādānā cetaso adhihānābhinivesānusayā,  in regard to whatever in the world are selfish means and attachments, or mental determinations, settled beliefs, and tendencies,  te pajahanto, viramati, na upādiyanto.1  giving these up, not being attached, he abstains (from them). parimukha sati upahapetvā, and establishing mindfulness at the front,  so sato va assasati, sato passasati.  mindful he breathes in, mindful he breathes out.   Dīgha vā assasanto “dīgha assasāmī” ti pajānāti, While breathing in long, he knows “I am breathing in long”,  dīgha vā passasanto “dīgha passasāmī” ti pajānāti, while breathing out long, he knows “I am breathing out long”,  rassa vā assasanto “rassa assasāmī” ti pajānāti, while breathing in short, he knows “I am breathing in short”,  rassa vā passasanto “rassa passasāmī” ti pajānāti. while breathing out short, he knows “I am breathing out short”.  Sabbakāyapaisavedī assasissāmī ti sikkhati, Fully aware of the whole body I will breathe in, like this he trains,  sabbakāyapaisavedī passasissāmī ti sikkhati, fully aware of the whole body I will breathe out, like this he trains,  passambhaya kāyasa=khāra assasissāmī ti sikkhati,  calming the bodily process I will breathe in, like this he trains,  passambhaya kāyasa=khāra passasissāmī ti sikkhati.  calming the bodily process I will breathe out, like this he trains. #### Eva me suta: Thus I have heard:

#### The Four Noble Truths  i.  Ida kho pana bhikkhave dukkha ariyasacca:

#### Now this, monks, is the noble truth of suffering:

jāti pi dukkhā birth is suffering

jarā pi dukkhā  also old age is suffering

vyādhi pi dukkho  also sickness is suffering

mara"am-pi dukkha  also death is suffering

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

appiyehi sampayogo dukkho  being joined to what is not dear is suffering

piyehi vippayogo dukkho  being separated from what is dear is suffering

yam-piccha na labhati tam-pi dukkha  also not to obtain what one longs for is suffering

sa=khittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā.  in brief, the five constituent groups (of mind and body) that are attached to are suffering.

ii.  Ida kho pana bhikkhave dukkhasamudaya1 ariyasacca:    Now this, monks, is the noble truth of the arising of suffering:  yā ya ta"hā ponobhavikā,  it is that craving which leads to continuation in existence,  nandirāgasahagatā, tatratatrābhinandinī, seyyathīda:  which is connected with enjoyment and passion, greatly enjoying this and that, as follows:

kāmata"hā  craving for sense pleasures

bhavata"hā  craving for continuation

vibhavata"hā.  craving for discontinuation.

iii.  Ida kho pana bhikkhave dukkhanirodha2 ariyasacca:

#### Now this, monks, is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering:

Realisation  i.  “Ida dukkha ariyasaccan”-ti -   “This is the noble truth of suffering” -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.   Ta kho pan’ “ida dukkha ariyasacca” pariññeyyan-ti - Now that to which “this is the noble truth of suffering” refers (i.e. suffering itself) ought to be fully known -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.   Ta kho pan’ “ida dukkha ariyasacca” pariññātan-ti - Now that to which “this is the noble truth of suffering” refers has been fully known -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

ii.  “Ida dukkhasamudaya ariyasaccan”-ti -   “This is the noble truth of the arising of suffering” -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.    Ta kho pan’ “ida dukkhasamudaya ariyasacca” pahātabban-ti - Now that to which “this is the noble truth of the arising of suffering” refers (i.e. craving) ought to be given up -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.   Ta kho pan’ “ida dukkhasamudaya ariyasacca” pahīnan-ti - Now that to which “this is the noble truth of the arising of suffering” refers has been given up,  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

iii. “Ida dukkhanirodha ariyasaccan”-ti -   “This is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering” -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose. Ta kho pan’ “ida dukkhanirodha ariyasacca” sacchikatan-ti - Now that to which “this is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering” refers has been experienced -  me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.

me bhikkhave pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu  to me, monks, regarding these previously unheard-of things  cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi,  vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose. Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

cakkhu udapādi, ñā"a udapādi, vision arose, knowledge arose,  paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.  wisdom rose, understanding arose, light arose.

Declaring the Awakening  Yāva kīvañ-ca me bhikkhave imesu catusu ariya-saccesu  For as long as to me, monks, in regard to these four noble truths    - eva tiparivaa dvādasākāra -   - turned like this, in three ways, twelvefold -  yathābhūta ñā"adassana na suvisuddha ahosi,  knowledge and insight as it really is was not quite clear,  neva tāvāha bhikkhave sadevake loke samārake sabrahmake,  for that long, monks, I did not declare to the world with its gods, Màra, and Brahma,  sassama"abrāhma"iyā pajāya sadevamanussāya,  to this generation, with its ascetics and brahmins, princes and men,  anuttara sammāsambodhi Abhisambuddho paccaññāsi.  that I was a Full and Perfect Sambuddha with unsurpassed complete awakening.  Yato ca kho me bhikkhave imesu catusu ariyasaccesu  But when to me, monks, in regard to these four noble truths    - eva tiparivaa dvādasākāra -   - turned like this, in three ways, twelvefold -  yathābhūta ñā"adassana suvisuddha ahosi,  knowledge and insight as it really is was quite clear  athāha bhikkhave sadevake loke samārake sabrahmake  then, monks, I did declare to the world with its gods, Màra, and Brahma,  sassama"abrāhma"iyā pajāya sadevamanussāya,  to this generation, with its ascetics and brahmins, princes and men,

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

anuttara sammāsambodhi Abhisambuddho paccaññāsi.  that I was a Full and Pefrect Sambuddha with unsurpassed complete awakening.  Ñā"añ-ca pana me dassana udapādi:  To me knowledge and seeing arose:

Akuppā me cetovimutti  Sure is my freedom of mind

ayam-antimā jāti  this is my last birth

natthi dāni punabbhavo” ti.  now there is no continuation of existence.”

The First Attainment

Idam-avoca Bhagavā, The Auspicious One said this,

attamanā pañcavaggiyā bhikkhū Bhagavato bhāsita abhinandun-ti.  and the group-of-five monks were uplifted and greatly rejoiced in what was said by the Auspicious One.  Imasmiñ-ca pana veyyākara"asmi bhaññamāne,  Moreover, as this sermon was being given,  āyasmato Ko"Jaññassa viraja, vītamala,  to venerable Koõóañña the dust-free, stainless,  Dhammacakkhu udapādi: Vision-of-the-Dhamma arose:

Ya kiñci samudayadhamma,  Whatever has the nature of arising,  sabban-ta1 nirodhadhamman-ti.  all that has the nature of ceasing.

The Gods of the Sense World  Pavattite ca pana Bhagavatā Dhammacakke  Now when the Dhamma Wheel was set rolling by the Auspicious One  Bhummā devā saddam-anussāvesu:  the Earth gods let loose a cry:  “Eta Bhagavatā Bārā"asiya Isipatane Migadāye, “The Auspicious One, while near Bàràõasã, in the Deer Park at Isipatana,  anuttara Dhammacakka pavattita, has set rolling the unsurpassed Dhamma Wheel,  appativattiya sama"ena vā brāhma"ena vā  and it cannot be rolled back by an ascetic or by a brahmin  devena vā mārena vā brahmunā vā kenaci vā lokasmin”-ti.  or by a god or by a demon or by a deity or by anyone in the world.”   Bhummāna devāna sadda sutvā  Having heard the cry of the Earth gods  Cātummahārājikā devā saddam-anussāvesu: the gods called the Four Great Kings let loose a cry:  “Eta Bhagavatā Bārā"asiya Isipatane Migadāye, “The Auspicious One, while near Bàràõasã, in the Deer Park at Isipatana,  anuttara Dhammacakka pavattita, has set rolling the unsurpassed Dhamma Wheel,  appativattiya sama"ena vā brāhma"ena vā  and it cannot be rolled back by an ascetic or by a brahmin  devena vā mārena vā brahmunā vā kenaci vā lokasmin”-ti.  or by a god or by a demon or by a deity or by anyone in the world.”  Cātummahārājikā devāna sadda sutvā  Having heard the cry of the gods called the Four Great Kings

23: Mahāsamayasutta

The Discourse on the Great Convention

#### Eva me suta:  Thus I have heard:

eka samaya Bhagavā Sakkesu viharati  at one time the Auspicious One was dwelling amongst the Sakyans  Kapilavatthusmi Mahāvane mahatā bhikkhusa=ghena saddhi in the Great Wood near Kapilavatthu with a great Saïgha of monks  pañcamattehi bhikkhusatehi sabbeheva Arahantehi, consisting of five hundred monks, all of them Worthy Ones,  dasahi ca lokadhātūhi1 devatā yebhuyyena sannipatitā honti and almost all of the gods from the ten world-elements had assembled  Bhagavanta dassanāya bhikkhusa=ghañ-ca.  to see the Auspicious One and the Saïgha of monks. 26. Parābhavasutta

The Discourse on Ruin

#### Eva me suta: Thus I have heard:

eka samaya Bhagavā Sāvatthiya viharati  at one time the Auspicious One was dwelling near Sàvatthi  Jetavane Anāthapi"Jikassa ārāme.  at Anàthapiõóika’s grounds in Jeta’s Wood. upasa=kamitvā Bhagavanta abhivādetvā ekam-anta ahāsi. and after approaching and worshipping the Auspicious One, he stood on one side.  Ekam-anta hitā kho sā devatā Bhagavanta gāthāya ajjhabhāsi: While standing on one side that god recited a verse to the Auspicious One:  1.   “Parābhavanta purisa ~ maya pucchāma Gotama - “We ask (reverend) Gotama regarding the man going to ruin -    Bhagavanta puham-āgamma, ~ ki parābhavato mukha?” we have come to ask the Auspicious One, ~ what is the cause of ruination?”   2.   “Suvijāno bhava hoti, ~ suvijāno parābhavo,  “The thriving man is easy to know, ~ the ruining man is easy to know,    Dhammakāmo bhava hoti, ~ Dhammadessī parābhavo.” the thriving man loves the Dhamma, ~ the ruining man hates the Dhamma.”   3.   “Iti heta vijānāma, ~ pahamo so parābhavo. “Indeed it is so, this we know, ~ this is the first ruin (of man).     Dutiya Bhagavā brūhi, ~ ki parābhavato mukha?” Please tell us, Auspicious One, what is the second cause of ruin?”   4.   “Asantassa piyā honti, ~ sante na kurute piya, “The bad are dear to him, ~ he does not endear the good,     asata Dhamma roceti, ~ ta parābhavato mukha.” he is pleased with the Dhamma of the bad, ~ that is a cause of ruination.”   5.   “Iti heta vijānāma, ~ dutiyo so parābhavo. “Indeed it is so, this we know, ~ this is the second ruin (of man).     Tatiya Bhagavā brūhi, ~ ki parābhavato mukha?” Please tell us, Auspicious One, what is the third cause of ruin?”

6. “Niddāsīlī sabhāsīlī, ~ anuhātā ca yo naro,  “That man who is fond of sleep, fond of society, unaroused,    alaso kodhapaññā"o, ~ ta parābhavato mukha.” lazy, and marked by anger, ~ that is a cause of ruination.”  7.   “Iti heta vijānāma, ~ tatiyo so parābhavo. “Indeed it is so, this we know, ~ this is the third ruin (of man). Chahama Bhagavā brūhi, ~ ki parābhavato mukha?” Please tell us, Auspicious One, what is the sixth cause of ruin?”

#### Eva me suta: Thus I have heard:

eka samaya Bhagavā Sāvatthiya viharati  at one time the Auspicious One was dwelling near Sàvatthi  Jetavane Anāthapi"Jikassa ārāme.  at Anàthapiõóika’s grounds in Jeta’s Wood. Sādhu me bhava Gotamo tathā Dhamma desetu  It would be good for me if the reverend Gotama would preach the Dhamma  yathāha jāneyya vasala vā vasalakara"e vā dhamme” ti.  so that I may know what an outcaste is, and what things make one an outcaste.”  “Tena hi brāhma"a su"āhi, sādhuka manasikarohi bhāsissāmī” ti. “Then listen, brahmin, apply your mind well, and I will speak.”  “Eva bho,” ti kho Aggikabhāradvājo brāhma"o  “Yes, reverend,” the brahmin Aggikabhàradvàja  Bhagavato paccassosi, Bhagavā etad-avoca:  replied to the Auspicious One, and the Auspicious One said this:   1.   “Kodhano upanāhī ca, ~ pāpamakkhī ca yo naro,  “That man who is angry, who has enmity, is bad, and insolent,    vipannadihi māyāvī, ~ ta jaññā vasalo iti. who has wrong views, and is deceitful, ~ he one should know as an outcaste.    2. 28: Saccavibha=gasutta

The Discourse giving the Analysis of the Truths

#### Eva me suta: Thus I have heard:

eka samaya Bhagavā Bārā"asiya viharati Isipatane Migadāye.  at one time the Auspicious One was dwelling near Bàràõasã in the Deer Park at Isipatana.  Tatra kho Bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesi: There it was that the Auspicious One addressed the monks, saying:  “Bhikkhavo!” ti, “Bhadante!” ti te bhikkhū Bhagavato paccassosu, “Monks!”, “Reverend Sir!” those monks replied to the Auspicious One,  Bhagavā etad-avoca: and the Auspicious One said this:

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

“Tathāgatena bhikkhave Arahatā Sammāsambuddhena, “The Realised One, monks, the Worthy One, the Perfect Sambuddha,  Bārā"asiya Isipatane Migadāye, while near Bàràõasã, in the Deer Park at Isipatana,  anuttara Dhammacakka pavattita, has set rolling the unsurpassed Dhamma Wheel,  appativattiya sama"ena vā brāhma"ena vā  and it cannot be rolled back by an ascetic or by a brahmin  devena vā mārena vā brahmunā vā kenaci vā lokasmi. or by a god or by a demon or by a deity or by anyone in the world.

Yad-ida catunna ariyasaccāna ācikkhanā,  ˚ That is to say, the explanation, revealing, making known, setting forth,  desanā, paññāpanā,1 pahapanā, vivara"ā, vibhajanā, uttānīkamma.  opening up, analysing, and making plain, of the four noble truths.

Katamesa catunna? Of which four?

Dukkhassa ariyasaccassa ācikkhanā,  ˚ The explanation, revealing, making known, setting forth,  desanā, paññāpanā, pahapanā, vivara"ā, vibhajanā, uttānīkamma.  opening up, analysing, and making plain, of the noble truth of suffering.  Dukkhasamudayassa ariyasaccassa ācikkhanā,  ˚ The explanation, revealing, making known, setting forth,  desanā, paññāpanā, pahapanā, vivara"ā, vibhajanā, uttānīkamma.  opening up, analysing, and making plain, of the noble truth of the arising of suffering.  Dukkhanirodhassa ariyasaccassa ācikkhanā,  ˚ The explanation, revealing, making known, setting forth,

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

desanā, paññāpanā, pahapanā, vivara"ā, vibhajanā, uttānīkamma.  opening up, analysing, and making plain, of the noble truth of the cessation of suffering. The First Noble Truth and its Analysis  Katamañ-cāvuso dukkha ariyasacca? Now what, venerable friends, is the noble truth of suffering?

Jāti pi dukkhā Birth is suffering  jarā pi dukkhā also old age is suffering  vyādhi pi dukkho1 also sickness is suffering  mara"am-pi dukkha also death is suffering  sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā pi dukkhā2 also grief, lamentation, pain, sorrow, and despair, is suffering  yam-piccha na labhati tam-pi dukkha3 also not to obtain what one longs for is suffering

1 CBhp: omits this line 2 MPP, PPV: -upāyāsā dukkhā 3 PPV2 omits this line.

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

sa=khittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā. in brief, the five constituent groups (of mind and body) that are attached to are suffering.

Katamā cāvuso jāti? Now what, venerable friends, is birth?  Yā tesa tesa sattāna tamhi tamhi sattanikāye  For the various beings in the various classes of beings  jāti, sañjāti, okkanti, abhinibbatti;1  there is birth, being born, appearing, turning up;  khandhāna pātubhāvo, āyatanāna pailābho:  the manifestation of the constituent groups (of mind and body), the acquisition of the sense spheres:  aya vuccatāvuso jāti. this venerable friends, is called birth.   Katamā cāvuso jarā? Now what, venerable friends, is old age?  Yā tesa tesa sattāna tamhi tamhi sattanikāye  For the various beings in the various classes of beings  jarā, jīra"atā, kha"Jicca, pālicca, valittacatā;  there is old age, agedness, broken teeth, greying hair, and wrinkled skin;  āyuno sahāni, indriyāna paripāko: the dwindling away of the life span, the decay of the sense faculties:  aya vuccatāvuso jarā. this venerable friends, is called old age.

1 CBhp: abhinibbanti

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

Katamañ-cāvuso mara"a? Now what, venerable friends, is death?  Ya tesa tesa sattāna tamhā tamhā sattanikāye  For the various beings in the various classes of beings  cuti, cavanatā, bhedo, antaradhāna, maccu, mara"a, kālakiriyā;  there is a fall, a falling away, a breaking up, a disappearance, a dying, a death, a making of time;  khandhāna bhedo, kalebarassa nikkhepo: the break up of the constituent groups (of mind and body), the throwing off of the body,  ida vuccatāvuso mara"a. this, venerable friends, is called death.   Katamo cāvuso soko? Now what, venerable friends, is grief?  Yo kho āvuso aññataraññatarena vyasanena samannāgatassa, For he who has, venerable friends, some sort of misfortune or other,  aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuhassa, who is touched by some sort of pain or another,  soko, socanā, socitatta, antosoko, antoparisoko: there is grief, grieving, the state of grieving, inner grief, great inner grief:  aya vuccatāvuso soko. this, venerable friends, is called grief.   Katamo cāvuso paridevo? Now what, venerable friends, is lamenation?  Yo kho āvuso aññataraññatarena vyasanena samannāgatassa, For he who has, venerable friends, some sort of misfortune or other,  aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuhassa, who is touched by some sort of pain or another,

ādevo, paridevo, ādevanā, paridevanā, ādevitatta, paridevitatta: there are laments, great laments, lamenting, great lamenting, the state of lamenting, great lamentation:  aya vuccatāvuso paridevo. this, venerable friends, is called lamentation.   Katamañ-cāvuso dukkha? Now what, venerable friends, is pain?  Ya kho āvuso kāyika dukkha, kāyika asāta,1 That, venerable friends, which is bodily pain, bodily disagreeableness,  kāyasamphassaja dukkha, asāta vedayita: pain born of contact with the body, disagreeable feeling:  ida vuccatāvuso dukkha. this, venerable friends, is called pain.   Katamañ-cāvuso domanassa? Now what, venerable friends, is sorrow?  Ya kho āvuso cetasika dukkha, cetasika asāta,2 That, venerable friends, which is mental pain, mental disagreeableness,  vedayita manosamphassaja dukkha, asāta vedayita: pain born of contact with the mind, disagreeable feeling:  ida vuccatāvuso domanassa.  this, venerable friends, is called sorrow.   Katamo cāvuso upāyāso? Now what, venerable friends, is despair?

Yo kho āvuso aññataraññatarena vyasanena samannāgatassa, For he who has, venerable friends, some sort of misfortune or other,  aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuhassa, who is touched by some sort of pain or another,  āyāso, upāyāso, āyāsitatta, upāyāsitatta: there is desponding, despairing, despondency, despair:  aya vuccatāvuso upāyāso. this, venerable friends, is called despair.   Katamañ-cāvuso yam-piccha na labhati tam-pi dukkha? Now what, venerable friends, is the suffering from not obtaining what one longs for?  Jātidhammāna āvuso sattāna eva icchā uppajjati:  To those beings subject to birth, venerable friends, this longing arises:

“Aho vata maya na jātidhammā assāma,  “Oh, might we not be subject to birth,  na ca vata no jāti āgaccheyyā!” ti  and may birth surely not come to us!”

Na kho paneta icchāya pattabba: But that cannot be attained merely by longing for it:  ida pi yam-piccha na labhati tam-pi dukkha.  this is the suffering from not obtaining what one longs for.   Jarādhammāna āvuso sattāna eva icchā uppajjati:  To those beings subject to old age, venerable friends, this longing arises:

“Aho vata maya na jarādhammā assāma,  “Oh, might we not be subject to old age,  na ca vata no jarā āgaccheyyā!” ti  may old age surely not come to us!”

Katamā1 cāvuso sa=khittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā? Now what, venerable friends, in brief, are the constituent groups (of mind and body) that are attached to that are suffering?

#### Seyyathīda:  They are as follows:

rūpūpādānakkhandho the form constituent group that is attached to   vedanūpādānakkhandho  the feelings constituent group that is attached to   saññūpādānakkhandho the perceptions constituent group that is attached to

Atirekàni Sattasuttàni

sa=khārūpādānakkhandho the (mental) processes constituent group that is attached to   viññā"ūpādānakkhandho.  the consciousness constituent group that is attached to.

Ime vuccantāvuso sa=khittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā. These, venerable friends, in brief, are called the constituent groups (of mind and body) that are attached to and that are suffering.  Ida vuccatāvuso dukkha ariyasacca. This, venerable friends, is called the noble truth of suffering.

The Second Noble Truth  Katamañ-cāvuso, dukkhasamudaya1 ariyasacca? Now what, venerable friends, is the noble truth of the arising of suffering?  Yā ya ta"hā ponobhavikā,  It is that craving which leads to continuation in existence,  nandirāgasahagatā, tatratatrābhinandinī, seyyathīda:  which is connected with enjoyment and passion, greatly enjoying this and  that, as follows:

kāmata"hā  craving for sense pleasures  bhavata"hā  craving for continuation  vibhavata"hā.  craving for discontinuation.

Ida vuccatāvuso dukkhasamudaya ariyasacca. This, venerable friends, is called the noble truth of the arising of suffering.

The Third Noble Truth  Katamañ-cāvuso dukkhanirodha1 ariyasacca? Now what, venerable friends, is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering?  Yo tassā yeva ta"hāya asesavirāganirodho - It is the complete fading away and cessation without remainder of that craving -  cāgo, painissaggo, mutti, anālayo.  liberation, letting go, release, and non-adherence.    Ida vuccatāvuso dukkhanirodha ariyasacca. This, venerable friends, is called the noble truth of the cessation of suffering.

The Fourth Noble Truth and its Analysis  Katamañ-cāvuso dukkhanirodhagāminī paipadā ariyasacca? Now what, venerable friends, is the noble truth of the practice going to the end of suffering?  Ayam-eva ariyo aha=giko maggo, seyyathīda: It is this noble path with eight factors, as follows:

right concentration.  Katamā cāvuso sammādihi? Now what, venerable friends, is right view?

Ya kho āvuso dukkhe ñā"a That, venerable friends, which is knowledge about suffering  dukkhasamudaye ñā"a knowledge about the arising of suffering  dukkhanirodhe ñā"a knowledge about the cessation of suffering  dukkhanirodhagāminiyā paipadāya1 ñā"a. knowledge about the practice going to the cessation of suffering.

Musāvādā verama"ī Refraining from false speech  pisu"āvācā1 verama"ī refraining from malicious speech  pharusāvācā2 verama"ī refraining from rough speech  samphappalāpā verama"ī. refraining from frivolous talk.

Pā"ātipātā verama"ī Refraining from killing living creatures  adinnādānā verama"ī refraining from taking what has not been given  kāmesu micchācārā verama"ī. refraining from sexual misconduct.

Katamo cāvuso sammā-ājīvo? Now what, venerable friends, is right livelihood?  Idhāvuso ariyasāvako micchā-ājīva pahāya, Here, venerable friends, a noble disciple, having given up wrong ways of livelihood,   sammā-ājīvena jīvika kappeti. earns his living by a right way of livelihood.  Aya vuccatāvuso sammā-ājīvo. This, venerable friends, is called right livelihood.   Katamo cāvuso sammāvāyāmo? Now what, venerable friends, is right endeavour? (in this regard) he endeavours, instigates energy, exerts his mind, and makes an effort. (in this regard) he endeavours, instigates energy, exerts his mind, and makes an effort. vāyamati viriya ārabhati citta pagga"hāti padahati. (in this regard) he endeavours, instigates energy, exerts his mind, and makes an effort. (in this regard) he endeavours, instigates energy, exerts his mind, and makes  an effort.  Aya vuccatāvuso sammāvāyāmo. This, venerable friends, is called right endeavour.   Katamā cāvuso sammāsati? (thus) he dwells having attained the third absorption. Ida vuccatāvuso dukkhanirodhagāminī paipadā ariyasacca. This, venerable friends, is called the noble truth of the practice going to the cessation of suffering.

Tathāgatena āvuso Arahatā Sammāsambuddhena, The Realised One, friends, the Worthy One, the Perfect Sambuddha,  Bārā"asiya Isipatane Migadāye, while near Bàràõasã, in the Deer Park at Isipatana,  anuttara Dhammacakka pavattita, has set rolling the unsurpassed Dhamma Wheel,  appativattiya sama"ena vā brāhma"ena vā  and it cannot be rolled back by an ascetic or by a brahmin  devena vā mārena vā brahmunā vā kenaci vā lokasmi.1 or by a god or by a demon or by a deity or by anyone in the world.

Yad-ida imesa catunna ariyasaccāna ācikkhanā,  ˚ That is to say, the explanation, revealing, making known, setting forth,  desanā, paññāpanā, pahapanā, vivara"ā, vibhajanā, uttānīkamman”-ti.  opening up, analysing, and making plain, of these four noble truths.”

Idam-avoc’2 āyasmā Sāriputto,  Venerable Sàriputta said this,

attamanā te bhikkhū āyasmato Sāriputtassa bhāsita abhinandun-ti. and those monks were uplifted and greatly rejoiced in venerable Sàriputta’s words.

The âñànàñiya Discourse

The Conclusion

Namo tassa Bhagavato Arahato Sammāsambuddhassa Reverence to him, the Auspicious One, the Worthy One, the Perfect Sambuddha
